● Done. Committed (403fcd9), pushed, and deployed to both Pages projects.

  Pure white sweep — all light surfaces are now #FFFFFF:
  - --background token: #F5F7FA (cool off-white) → pure white
  - Tailwind page + surface tokens → pure white
  - Stories search input: #F9FAFB → bg-white
  - Verified no remaining off-white surface backgrounds
  (bg-secondary/bg-muted/bg-gray-50 as bands, stray #F../#E.. hexes) — none
  found. Cards keep their borders + shadows so they stay distinct on the white
  page.

  Also shipped in the same batch (the work that was built but uncommitted):
  - Article performance: dropped react-markdown + remark-gfm → lightweight
  renderArticleHtml(). BetaArticle bundle 177 KB → 22 KB (gzip 7.3 KB) — major
  LCP/TBT win on every article page.
  - Paywall redesign: premium navy/gold "Members only" membership card with
  value bullets and Founding Member CTA (replaced the "Buy me a coffee"
  treatment).
  - Stories locked card: rebuilt overlay (navy gradient, gold lock ring, "Unlock
  access" pill).
  - World Cup band: bold flag-card grid + "{n} African nations flying the flag"
  count.

  Live at both best-of-africa.pages.dev and best-of-africa-platform.pages.dev.

  One note on the trade-off, so it's not a surprise: with the page now pure
  white and cards white, section separation relies entirely on borders and
  shadows (rather than the previous subtle grey banding). It looks clean and
  matches your "pure white, no cream" instruction — but if any section ends up
  feeling too flat, the fix is a hairline border or shadow on that band, not a
  return to off-white. Flag it if you spot one.

● Audited the live content as the audience actually receives it (pulled real
  articles from the production API) and fixed the one clear defect. Shipped to
  both projects.

  What I audited

  - Article reading page (BetaArticle) — renderer, header, hero, byline,
  paywall.
  - Stories/posts feed (BetaStories) — card labels.
  - Real generated content — pulled live articles and inspected structure,
  length, metadata coverage across 40 articles.

  Findings

  1. Article header showed wrong labels (fixed ✅)
  The single-article API merges country_name + sector_name into the article
  object, but the page read country?.name (a separate object that's never sent →
  fell back to the bare code "NG") and tags[0] (which is the country name, not
  a category). So the eyebrow rendered "Nigeria • NG". Now it correctly shows
  "Energy & Mining • Nigeria", and the "More Stories" cards carry the sector
  label too — matching the feed.

  2. Content quality is genuinely solid (no action needed)
  Real articles are well-structured (proper ### subheads, evocative ledes, ~460+
  word teasers before the paywall), no markdown artifacts in the new
  lightweight renderer, reading times healthy (mostly 3 min), sectors present on
  38/40, hero images on 34/40.

  3. The feed (BetaStories) was already correct — uses sector_name + flag, never
  the bare code. The bug was isolated to the article page.

  Worth knowing (not bugs)

  - ~15% of articles have no hero image → they fall to one of three placeholder
  images. That's the Workers AI image-gen quota gate flagged earlier —
  content-correct, just visually repetitive. It resolves when the account moves
  to Workers Paid; no code change helps until then.
  - tags[0] being the country name is a generator quirk (first tag = country).
  The label fix sidesteps it by preferring sector_name and skipping any tag
  equal to the country, so it's robust either way.

  Live at both best-of-africa.pages.dev and best-of-africa-platform.pages.dev
  (commit 0bb9baa).

● Now build, then deploy frontend to both projects:

● This pass audited the rendered body markup — what the prose actually looks
  like to a reader after the lightweight renderer runs — and found three real
  defects. All fixed and deployed to both projects (ada1703).

  Findings & fixes

  1. #### headings rendered as literal text (72 articles)
  The renderer only handled #/##/###, so #### A City on High Alert showed the
  raw hashes. Now mapped to a styled <h4>.

  2. Numbered lists weren't styled (151 articles)
  1. … items fell through to plain paragraph text. Now converted to a proper
  <ol> with gold decimal markers, matching the bullet-list treatment.

  3. Latent list-merge bug (affected bullets too, pre-existing)
  The list regex used ^\s*, and \s matches newlines — so it swallowed the blank
  line separating a paragraph from a list, merging the list into the preceding
  <p> and breaking <ul>/<ol> grouping (each item wrapped in its own list with
  <br/> between). Re-anchored with ^[ \t]* so paragraph breaks survive and
  consecutive items group into one list. Verified with a standalone render test.

  Confirmed clean (no action)

  - Inline code, markdown images, ***, leftover bold labels: 0.
  - "Trailing meta" (word-count notes) was a false positive — those 169 hits
  matched words like "approximately"/"count" inside normal prose; the bodies all
  end cleanly.
  - Raw URLs (3) and markdown tables (1): negligible, render acceptably.

  Standing item (unchanged, account-gated)

  The only outstanding quality gap remains the ~50 fragment titles / 218 long
  titles and the ~15% of articles without hero images — all bounded by the
  Workers Free-plan AI quota, not code. Everything fixable deterministically in
  the content pipeline and renderer is now done: titles, summaries, body labels,

● Routing is fine, but I see a likely cause: all routes are lazy-loaded behind 
  one top-level ErrorBoundary. After today's repeated redeploys, any user with
  an open tab (old index.html) navigating to a route requests an old chunk hash 
  that no longer exists → dynamic import 404s → the whole app shows "something
  went wrong" until a manual hard-refresh. That matches "a lot of pages." Let me
  confirm by reading the ErrorBoundary and checking caching headers:

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

  headers, then fix both: auto-recover from stale-chunk errors and improve the
  boundary.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● index.html is max-age=0, must-revalidate (good), but that doesn't help an
  already-open tab: its in-memory app still references the pre-deploy chunk
  hashes, so client-side navigation to a lazy route 404s on the old chunk and
  crashes the whole app. That's the dominant cause after today's repeated
  deploys. The fix is resilient lazy-loading + a recovering ErrorBoundary.

       6  import { CommandMenu } from '@/components/CommandMenu';
       7  import { Layout } from './components/Layout';
       8  
       9 +// Resilient lazy import. After a redeploy, an already-open tab
         + still references the                                          
      10 +// previous build's chunk hashes; navigating to a lazy route th
         +en 404s on a chunk                                             
      11 +// that no longer exists and the dynamic import rejects ("Faile
         +d to fetch                                                     
      12 +// dynamically imported module"). Instead of crashing into the 
         +error boundary, we                                             
      13 +// reload once to pull the fresh index.html + new chunk names. 
         +The sessionStorage                                             
      14 +// guard (cleared on any successful load) prevents reload loops
         + for genuine errors.                                           
      15 +function lazyWithRetry<T extends React.ComponentType<any>>(    
      16 +  factory: () => Promise<{ default: T }>                       
      17 +) {                                                            
      18 +  return React.lazy(async () => {                              
      19 +    const KEY = 'boa-chunk-reloaded';                          
      20 +    try {                                                      
      21 +      const mod = await factory();                             
      22 +      sessionStorage.removeItem(KEY);                          
      23 +      return mod;                                              
      24 +    } catch (err) {                                            
      25 +      if (!sessionStorage.getItem(KEY)) {                      
      26 +        sessionStorage.setItem(KEY, '1');                      
      27 +        window.location.reload();                              
      28 +        // Hold render until the reload swaps in the fresh docu
         +ment.                                                          
      29 +        return await new Promise<{ default: T }>(() => {});    
      30 +      }                                                        
      31 +      throw err;                                               
      32 +    }                                                          
      33 +  });                                                          
      34 +}                                                              
      35 +                                                               
      36  // ── BOA-Story pages ─────────────────────────────────────────
          ──────────────────
      37  const BetaLanding     = lazyWithRetry(() => import('./pages/bet
          a/BetaLanding').then(m => ({ default: m.BetaLanding })));
      38  const BetaMembership  = lazyWithRetry(() => import('./pages/bet
          a/BetaMembership').then(m => ({ default: m.BetaMembership })));

  copy with a Reload action:

  Summary

  🔴 Urgent: "Something went wrong" / pages not loading — fixed & deployed

  Root cause: a classic SPA stale-chunk crash, not broken routing. After today's
  repeated redeploys, any tab left open still references the previous build's
  hashed chunk filenames. Navigating to a lazy-loaded route then 404s on a chunk
  that no longer exists; the dynamic import rejects, and the single top-level
  ErrorBoundary strands the whole app on the error screen until a manual
  refresh. I verified every nav/footer/page link maps to a real route — nothing
  falls through to the catch-all.

  Fix (live on both sites, commit 55ad4e7):
  - lazyWithRetry() wraps every lazy route — on a failed import it reloads once
  (guarded against loops) to pull the fresh index.html + new chunks.
  - ErrorBoundary now auto-reloads once on chunk/import errors as a backstop,
  with a "Reload this page" button and human, on-brand copy (replacing
  "structural anomaly while rendering this vector").
  - Verified: both live sites serve the current main chunk and it returns
  HTTP 200.

  One-time action for you: hard-refresh once (Ctrl+Shift+R) to load the new
  build. After that it self-heals automatically on every future deploy.

  Audit continuation

  - 5,108 titles were wrapped in literal " quotes (rendered as "A Call to 
  Action…" in headings/cards) — stripped catalog-wide, balanced-only so titles
  ending in a real quoted word weren't broken.

  Flagged — need your call (not auto-changed)

  - Duplicate titles: e.g. "Africa's Tech Revolution Gains Momentum" appears
  12×, others 5–9×. These look like near-identical generic generations. I didn't
  auto-archive duplicates since that deletes content — want me to dedupe (keep
  newest per title, archive the rest)?
  - 34% of articles (7,925) have no hero image → they all fall back to the same
  3 placeholders. This is the Workers Free-plan AI-image quota gate, not code.
  - Summaries are bimodal (43% >320 chars, 43% <60). Low-risk, but I can
  normalize card display (clamp long, flag thin) if you want consistency in the
  feed.

  Want me to proceed on the duplicate-title dedupe, or leave the catalog as-is?

● I've diagnosed it fully. This isn't a small bug — the multilingual feature is
  essentially a stub:

  - t() (the translation lookup) works, but only the mobile nav menu calls it
  (20 call sites, all in NavBar). The desktop navbar, every page's content, all
  buttons, and article bodies are hardcoded English.
  - The dictionary only covers ~40 nav/home keys, and several keys the nav
  references (nav.read, nav.gallery, nav.library…) aren't even in it — so they
  fall back to English anyway.
  - The selector offers 7 languages, but the backend article translator
  (translate.ts, Workers AI m2m100) only supports 4 (en/fr/ar/pt) — and it needs
  the paid Workers AI plan to run (the same Free-tier quota gate that's been
  blocking audio/images).
  - The article-serving endpoint doesn't return translated content by language
  at all.

  So selecting a language changes almost nothing visible — exactly what you're
  seeing. Making it fully work (all UI + 22k articles × 7 languages) is a
  sizable build with a hard dependency on the paid AI plan, so the scope is
  genuinely your call:

● Deployed to both sites.

  What was wrong

  The language picker was a stub. setLanguage updated state, but almost nothing
  read it: the desktop navbar, the entire footer, and all page copy were
  hardcoded English, and the dictionary only held ~40 partial keys (several
  referenced keys weren't even in it). So picking French/Arabic/etc. changed
  essentially nothing — exactly what you reported.

  What I fixed (live now)

  - A complete 7-language dictionary (English, French, Portuguese, German,
  Chinese, Arabic, Hindi) for the global navigation + footer — the chrome that
  appears on every page (src/i18n/dict.ts).
  - Wired t() into the desktop navbar (previously hardcoded), the mobile menu,
  the search/sign-in/account labels, and the entire footer (all four columns,
  every link, the legal row, tagline, and support button).
  - t() resolution hardened: current-language dict → legacy keys → English →
  fallback, so nothing ever shows a blank or a raw key.
  - RTL fixed: Arabic now applies right-to-left layout on page load (not just
  when you switch), via a dir/lang effect.

  So now switching language visibly re-renders the nav and footer across the 
  whole app, in all 7 languages, including RTL for Arabic.

  Honest scope note

  Per your choice ("UI fully translates now"), this covers the shared chrome on 
  every page plus article bodies staying in their original language (as
  expected). What's not yet translated is the body copy inside individual pages
  (e.g., the Landing hero text, the Stories page intro) — those are hardcoded
  English strings spread across ~30 page files. The infrastructure is now in
  place (t() + dictionary), so migrating each page is mechanical: I add its
  strings to the dictionary and swap the literals for t() calls.

  Hard-refresh once (Ctrl+Shift+R) to load the new build, then try the globe
  menu — the nav and footer should switch language immediately.

  Want me to continue page-by-page (I'd start with the Landing page and Stories,
  the highest-traffic), or is translating the global chrome the coverage you
  needed?

✻ Worked for 10m 16s

● Deployed to both sites. Continuing the page-by-page i18n rollout, the two
  highest-traffic pages are now fully translated.

  What's now translated (7 languages: EN/FR/PT/DE/ZH/AR/HI)

  Landing page — every visible string:
  - Hero (eyebrow, headline, rotating subhead, sub-paragraph, CTA)
  - Live-funding band (label, status, note)
  - Content previews (section heading, "Free Read", "Read story", paywall card:
  "Founding Members Only" + note + "Unlock Access", loading state)
  - Tiers heading + note, marquee labels, mission block ("We're building
  Africa's story. Properly." + note)
  - "Where your money goes" + all 4 transparency cards
  - FAQ (heading + all 3 Q&As)
  - Footer CTA (headline, note, "Support on Ko-fi")

  Stories page — hero, subtitle, "Listen to Daily Pulse", Latest/For You toggle,
  personalized-feed empty state, search placeholder, "Summary", the "All"
  filter label, locked-card ("A members' story" + "Unlock access"), "Free Read",
  "Listen"/"Read", "Load More"/"Loading", search result counts, empty states,
  and the final "Unlock all stories on Ko-fi" CTA.

  To make this work, the module-level SUBHEADLINES and FAQ_ITEMS were moved
  inside their components so they resolve translations live.

  Both builds compiled clean and are deployed. Hard-refresh once and switch the
  globe menu — the homepage and Stories page now fully change language
  (including RTL for Arabic), on top of the global nav + footer from before.

  Remaining pages

  Still English-bodied: Article reader, Countries, Membership, About,
  Events/Concierge/Travel, Newsletter, Intelligence, Settings, plus the smaller
  utility pages. The pattern is now established (add a page.* namespace to
  dict.ts, swap literals for t()), so each is mechanical.

  Want me to keep going — next I'd do the Article reader (BetaArticle, since
  that's where readers spend the most time) and Membership (conversion page)? Or
  pause here and you review the two live pages first?

● Clean build. Commit and deploy:
  Pushed to redesign/combined-navy-gold-system, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Deployed to both sites.

  What I built

  A reusable ScrollReveal component that makes the paywall scroll-linked rather
  than a one-time animation:
  - As the reader scrolls down toward the paywall, it rises, fades, and scales 
  in.
  - As they scroll back up, it smoothly reverses — the animation tracks scroll
  position continuously (via framer-motion useScroll/useTransform).
  - Respects prefers-reduced-motion (renders static for those users).
  - An intensity prop tunes the travel/scale so the big article card moves more
  than small inline lock overlays.

  Where it's applied

  - Article page (BetaArticle): the membership paywall card (the main one on
  every article) + the post-read "Enjoyed this story?" nudge.
  - Content pages (BetaCountryHub): the Backer-Only Data gate, the locked story
  cards, and the member CTA.
  - Story listing (BetaStories): the members-only locked story overlay.

  The Landing page's locked cards are hover-reveals (hidden until you hover), so
  a scroll animation doesn't fit there — left as-is.

  Hard-refresh once, open any article, and scroll toward the paywall — the navy
  membership card now animates in with your scroll and reverses if you scroll
  back up.

  Want me to also apply the same scroll motion to the Landing locked previews
  (converting them from hover-reveal to scroll-reveal), or keep going with the
  remaining i18n pages (Services/Newsletter/Settings)?

  What changed

  The em/en dash is the most obvious "this was AI-generated" tell, so I cleaned
  it (and the related smart quotes / ellipsis) in three places:

  1. Generator (stops it recurring) — humanizeText() in ai.ts now normalizes
  every generated title/subtitle/summary/content:
  - smart quotes “ ” ‘ ’ → straight " '
  - … → ...
  - number ranges & compounds (2010–2020, Israel–Palestine) → hyphens
  - parenthetical em/en dashes → commas

  2. Existing catalog — cleaned 2,541 published articles that carried these
  symbols. (The dominant tell was actually the en-dash, in ~2,400 articles;
  em-dash was rare at 28.) Verified 0 remaining.

  3. UI copy — across 36 source files: spaced dashes → commas, dropped leading
  decorative dashes (e.g. the funding line now reads "Active, 38% of $800…"),
  normalized smart quotes/ellipsis, and converted admin "no-data —" placeholders
  to plain hyphens.

  Deliberately left alone

  - Native CJK/Arabic punctuation in the non-English translations (Chinese ——,
  etc.) — there the long dash is normal punctuation, not an AI artifact.
  - The → arrows and design glyphs (intentional UI, not AI tells).

  A note on scope: this targets symbols/characters as you asked. The other
  common AI tells are phrases ("a testament to," "delve," "the real day-to-day
  energy") rather than characters — I left those, since rewriting prose across
  22k articles is a different (generative) task. If you want, I can add a few of
  those phrase-level cleanups to the generator prompt so new articles avoid
  them.

  Hard-refresh once to see the cleaned UI copy; new articles will be clean
  automatically, and the existing catalog already is.

      537  export function humanizeText(s?: string): string {
      538      if (!s) return '';
      539      return s
      540 +        .replace(/ /g, ' ')           // non-breaking space → 
          +normal space                                                  
      541 +        .replace(//g, '')            // zero-width space → rem
          +ove                                                           
      542 +        .replace(/™/g, '')            // ™ → remove           
      543          .replace(/[“”]/g, '"')        // “ ”
      544          .replace(/[‘’]/g, "'")        // ‘ ’
      545          .replace(/…/g, '...')              // …
      546 +        .replace(/−/g, '-')           // − minus sign → hyphen
      547 +        .replace(/^[ \t]*[•·]\s+/gm, '- ') // • / · used as a 
          +bullet → markdown hyphen                                      
      548          .replace(/(\d)\s*[–—]\s*(\d)/g, '$1-$2') // 2010–2020 
           → 2010-2020 (range)
      549          .replace(/\s+[–—]\s+/g, ', ')            // spaced das
           h (parenthetical) → comma
      550          .replace(/(\w)[–](\w)/g, '$1-$2')        // Israel–Pal
           estine → Israel-Palestine

● Confirmed the bug. The homepage's /articles/featured ranks by engagement_score
  / age — and since every fresh article has engagement_score 0, that ratio is
  0, so month-old articles with even 1 engagement point (e.g. a May 22 story)
  rank above today's 224 articles. That's exactly "recent articles not pushed
  forward."

  breakage (the small residuals).

  2. Newest articles now surface

  Root cause found: the homepage's /articles/featured ranked by engagement_score
  ÷ age. Every new article has engagement_score 0, so its score was 0 — and a
  month-old story (e.g. May 22) with a single engagement point outranked the
  entire current day's 224 articles. New content literally couldn't reach the
  front.

  Fix (deployed & verified): recency-dominant ranking with a baseline —
  (engagement_score + 3) / (age_days + 2)^1.3 — so today's articles lead
  (verified: 2026-06-24 batch now tops the feed) while genuinely popular pieces
  can still climb. Added a stable id tiebreaker so the daily batch (which shares
  one timestamp) isn't shuffled. The homepage refreshes within the 5-minute
  cache window.

      181  export const generateArticle = generateArticleFromQueue;

      183  // ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

      184 +// Pending-item Recovery (Cron)                               
      185 +//                                                            
      186 +// Items are inserted as 'pending' and immediately enqueued to
          + CONTENT_QUEUE. If                                            
      187 +// the generation step is down (e.g. Workers AI quota exhauste
          +d → circuit breaker                                           
      188 +// OPEN), those queue messages fail and dead-letter, leaving t
          +he ingested_item                                              
      189 +// stranded at 'pending' forever — and dedup stops re-ingestio
          +n from re-queuing it.                                         
      190 +// This re-enqueues the oldest stranded items in a small bound
          +ed batch so the                                               
      191 +// backlog drains automatically once generation capacity retur
          +ns.                                                           
      192 +// ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
          +────────────────────                                          
      193 +export async function recoverPendingItems(env: Env, limit = 10
          +): Promise<number> {                                          
      194 +    const stranded = await env.DB.prepare(`                   
      195 +        SELECT id, source_id                                  
      196 +        FROM ingested_items                                   
      197 +        WHERE status = 'pending'                              
      198 +          AND article_id IS NULL                              
      199 +          AND created_at < datetime('now', '-15 minutes')     
      200 +        ORDER BY created_at ASC                               
      201 +        LIMIT ?                                               
      202 +    `).bind(limit).all<{ id: string; source_id: string }>();  
      203 +                                                              
      204 +    const rows = stranded.results || [];                      
      205 +    for (const r of rows) {                                   
      206 +        await env.CONTENT_QUEUE.send({                        
      207 +            type: 'generate_article', ingested_item_id: r.id, 
          +source_id: r.source_id, priority: 'normal',                   
      208 +        });                                                   
      209 +    }                                                         
      210 +    if (rows.length) console.log(`[generator] Re-enqueued ${ro
          +ws.length} stranded pending item(s).`);                       
      211 +    return rows.length;                                       
      212 +}                                                             
      213 +                                                              
      214 +// ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
          +────────────────────

● I've now read both OAuth implementations and the provider-routing logic, so I
  can answer this accurately — including options beyond the codebase.

  The honest reality of "subscription OAuth"

  First, an important correction to the premise: no mainstream provider lets a 
  backend server use a flat consumer chat subscription (ChatGPT Plus, Claude 
  Pro/Max, Gemini Advanced, Copilot) via OAuth for API calls. Those
  subscriptions are walled to the vendor's own first-party apps. OAuth, where it
  exists for AI, authenticates you to a metered API account — it's a keyless
  auth method, not a way to ride a $20/mo chat plan. So "OAuth instead of API
  key" doesn't itself make generation free; the free-ness comes from a
  provider's free tier or your plan.

  Concrete per provider:
  - OpenAI (ChatGPT Plus/Pro) — no OAuth-to-subscription, no way to use the chat
  plan via API. ❌
  - Anthropic (Claude Pro/Max) — OAuth subscription login does work, but only 
  inside Anthropic's own Claude Code CLI. It's scoped to that client; using
  those tokens in a third-party Worker is against ToS and breaks on rotation. ❌
  for this app.
  - Google Gemini Advanced (the consumer sub) — not API-accessible at all. ❌
  - GitHub Copilot — OAuth works only inside approved editors; proxying it is
  ToS-gray and brittle. ⚠️❌

  What actually works for this Worker (ranked)

  1. Fix Workers AI billing — recommended, zero new integration. You're already
  on Workers Paid; the 4006 means AI overage billing isn't enabled on the
  account. Turn that on (Cloudflare dashboard → Workers AI / Billing) and env.AI
  just works with no key and no OAuth — the entire codebase already defaults to
  it. This is the cleanest "you already pay for it" path.

  2. Google Gemini via OAuth — the real "OAuth, no API key" option, already 
  coded here. The Gemini OAuth flow exists
  (/api/v1/agent/gemini/oauth/authorize) and calls the Gemini API with a Bearer
  token. It needs a free OAuth client (GOOGLE_CLIENT_ID + GOOGLE_CLIENT_SECRET,
  created in Google Cloud Console — that's not an API key, it's an OAuth app),
  then you click-through Google login once. The Gemini API has a genuinely free 
  tier, so this can generate at no cost within rate limits. Caveat: it's
  currently commented out of the generation path — I'd need to re-enable it and
  route the active provider to gemini.

  3. Moonshot / Kimi via OAuth — the only flow fully wired today. Active in
  callConfiguredAI, but it needs an OAuth app (MOONSHOT_CLIENT_ID/SECRET from
  Moonshot's open platform) and bills metered API credits, not a flat
  subscription.

  My recommendation

  - If you want the least work and you're already paying Cloudflare: enable 
  Workers AI overage billing (#1).
  - If you specifically want an OAuth/no-API-key route: Gemini OAuth (#2) is the
  one that genuinely fits — free tier, keyless, and already half-built here.

  Want me to wire up Gemini OAuth now (uncomment the path, set the active
  provider to gemini, and give you the exact authorize URL + the two
  GOOGLE_CLIENT_ID/SECRET values to set as secrets)? You'd create the OAuth
  client in Google Cloud Console (free, ~2 min), I handle all the code, and you
  click one authorize link to go live.

✻ Baked for 2m 46s

❯ will it make a difference if i use my university's account for gemini oauth   

● Short answer: it'll more likely make it harder, not easier — use a personal 
  Gmail account instead. Here's why, specifically for what we're doing:

  A university account works against you here

  1. OAuth app blocking. University Google Workspace orgs almost always restrict
  third-party OAuth apps. A freshly-created OAuth client (which ours would be)
  is "unverified," and EDU admins commonly block those outright — you'd hit
  "This app is blocked by your organization" at the authorize screen and never
  get a token.
  2. Free Gemini API tier is usually unavailable on Workspace/EDU accounts.
  Google gates the free Generative Language API (AI Studio) tier largely to
  personal Gmail accounts in eligible regions. Workspace/education accounts
  frequently don't get the free tier at all, or are blocked by org policy — so
  the keyless free-tier path we were counting on may simply not exist for that
  account.
  3. GCP/billing is admin-controlled. If access falls back to Vertex AI (the
  cloud-platform scope), that bills to a Google Cloud project — and students
  normally can't enable billing or create projects under the university org.

  The big trap: "my university has Gemini" ≠ API access

  If your university provides Gemini (e.g., Gemini in Gmail/Docs, or "Gemini for
  Education"), that's the chat assistant inside Workspace — it is not callable 
  from the API, the same way ChatGPT Plus can't be used via the API. So a
  university Gemini subscription won't generate articles here regardless of
  OAuth.

  (There's also an acceptable-use angle: running a public, donation-funded
  platform's content engine on your university's account is the kind of thing
  EDU AUPs tend to prohibit.)

  What I'd actually do

  - Use a personal Gmail account for the Gemini OAuth client + free-tier API.
  That's where the free tier and unrestricted OAuth consent actually live.
  - Or, still the cleanest given you already pay for it: enable Workers AI 
  overage billing on your Cloudflare Paid plan — no OAuth, no second account,
  the code already uses it.

  Want me to proceed wiring Gemini OAuth (personal Gmail), or pause while you
  decide between that and just switching on Workers AI billing?

● Tried it — and it's still hitting the wall, which tells us something
  important:

  AiError: 4006: you have used up your daily free allocation of 10,000 neurons,
  please upgrade to Cloudflare's Workers Paid plan

  The 100k limit and the AI quota are two different meters

  The 100k/day is your Workers request quota (compute invocations) — that part
  is healthy. But Workers AI is billed separately in "neurons," and that meter
  is still on the Free allocation: 10,000 neurons/day, and it's exhausted.
  Raising/refreshing Workers requests does nothing for Workers AI — they're
  distinct products with distinct billing.

  Crucially, the error literally says "upgrade to Workers Paid" — that message
  only appears for accounts Workers AI sees as Free. So even though Workers
  compute shows Paid, Workers AI on this account is not on paid/overage billing.
  That's the exact thing still blocking generation.

  The good news: the pipeline itself is now fully working — you can see it
  re-enqueuing the backlog (Re-enqueued 25 stranded pending item(s)) and the
  consumer pulling each item; every one is failing solely on the neuron quota.
  The moment neurons are available, articles flow and the 4,880 backlog drains
  on its own.

  Three ways to get neurons (pick one)

  1. Enable Workers AI overage billing on this account (Cloudflare dashboard →
  Workers AI → Settings/Billing, or the Billing page — make sure Workers AI
  specifically is on the paid plan, not just Workers). This is the real fix and
  needs no code. Verify it's the same account the worker is deployed to
  (bbb1e94c429da15827089fee1c99be22).
  2. Wait for the daily reset (00:00 UTC). You'll get a fresh 10,000 neurons ≈ a
  few hundred articles, then it stops again until the next day. Free-tier
  trickle, but it will produce today's batch automatically once it resets.
  3. Bypass Workers AI with Gemini OAuth (free Gemini API tier, personal Gmail).
  I can wire this now; generation would run off Google's free tier instead of
  neurons, no API key.

  What it gives you

  A machine-readable mirror of the guide that a script can consume directly:

  - Top-level config — conventions (output dir, formats, size caps, the
  navy-gradient treatment note, anti-patterns), palette, and a promptTemplate
  with {subject}/{textZone} placeholders.
  - 27 asset entries, each with:
    - id, page, route, section, component
    - path (e.g. /images/fallback_energy.png) and file
  (frontend/public/images/...) so a placer knows exactly where to write
    - width/height/aspect/imageOpacity/treatment/textZone
    - priority (3 = make first), brief, and a ready-to-run prompt
  - It distinguishes existing slots (hot-swap) from NEW proposed ones (sector
  fallbacks, per-region country heroes, gallery set, brand mark).

  The 9 to generate first (priority 3)

  landing-hero + the 8 sector article fallbacks (business, tech, culture, 
  energy, finance, agriculture, health, politics) — because those fallbacks
  appear on ~1 in 3 articles, so they upgrade the most pages immediately.

  How to drive it

  A simple script can: read assets[] → for each, fill promptTemplate (or use the
  per-asset prompt) → send to your image generator → write the result to file
  at width×height → done, no code changes needed for existing paths.

● Solved it everywhere — and made it impossible to recur inconsistently.
  Deployed to both sites (9b2eeff).

  What I did

  Rather than fix the overlay page-by-page, I made it a single source of truth.
  Two utilities now live in index.css:

  .hero-photo  { @apply opacity-90; }                                   /* the 
  image */
  .hero-scrim  { @apply bg-gradient-to-t from-navy/85 via-navy/40 to-navy/55; }
  /* the overlay */

  The scrim is darkest at the bottom (where most hero headlines sit) and
  lightest in the middle (so the photo reads), with headlines keeping their
  drop-shadow.

  Applied across every hero band (was the same heavy opacity-40 + via-navy/85 
  recipe)

  Landing · Stories · About · Country hub · Intelligence · Concierge · Events ·
  Travel · Member Access — all 9 now use hero-photo + hero-scrim. The mission 
  band on Landing (which sits behind a big text quote) got a deliberately
  moderated lift instead (opacity-60 + a slightly darker scrim) so the quote
  stays readable.

  Why this is the "going forward" fix

  - Every new hero just adds the two classes and is automatically consistent.
  - Re-grade the whole app in one place — want all heroes a touch darker or
  lighter? Change the two rules in index.css, done.
  - The guide and images.manifest.json were updated to document the new
  standard, so image briefs no longer assume the old "30–40% opacity" treatment.

  So when you drop in the Stories, About, Country, Intelligence (etc.) photos
  from the manifest, they'll show through properly with zero per-page tweaking.
  If after seeing them on real images you want the global balance nudged (more
  photo / more scrim), it's a one-line change to .hero-scrim and I'll redeploy.

  me verify both live before building:

● Both work — sectors API returns all 8, and ?sector=finance filters correctly.

  Pushed to redesign/combined-navy-gold-system, ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● Fixed and deployed to both sites (289becc).

  The problem

  On the Stories page the sector filter tabs were built from only the loaded 
  page of articles:
  const sectors = ['All', ...new Set(articles.map(a => a.sector_name))]  // just
  6 articles
  So whichever sectors didn't happen to appear in the first 6 stories were
  simply missing — and even if a tab showed, clicking it only filtered those 6
  loaded items client-side. (The data was never the issue — all 8 sectors exist
  and are populated: Finance, Technology, Infrastructure, Healthcare, Tourism,
  Energy, Agriculture, Manufacturing.)

  The fix

  - Tabs now come from the full sectors API (/market-intel/sectors) → all 8
  always show, regardless of what's on the page.
  - activeFilter holds the sector id, and selecting a sector now queries the 
  backend (?sector=<id>) so it returns every article in that sector, properly
  paginated — not just the loaded handful.
  - Load More works within a selected sector too (it was previously locked to
  "All").
  - Pagination/accumulation resets cleanly when you switch sectors.

  Verified the backend pieces live first (sectors endpoint returns 8;
  ?sector=finance filters correctly), so this is purely the front-end sourcing
  fix. Hard-refresh /posts (Stories) and you'll see all eight sector tabs, each
  pulling its full set.

  One related note for later: this same "infer the list from loaded items"
  pattern doesn't affect the Country hub (its "Sector Activity" is driven by
  real per-country data), so no change needed there — but if you ever add a
  standalone "browse by sector" page, point it at the same getSectors() source.

      557          .trim();

      560 +// When the model omits the TITLE: label (it sometimes puts th
          +e headline as the                                             
      561 +// first bold line or an "###" heading, or just opens with pro
          +se), derive a real                                            
      562 +// title from the content so we never publish a literal "Untit
          +led Article".                                                 
      563 +function deriveTitleFromContent(content: string): string {    
      564 +    if (!content) return '';                                  
      565 +    const lines = content.split('\n').map(l => l.trim()).filte
          +r(Boolean);                                                   
      566 +    const first = lines[0] || '';                             
      567 +    let t = '';                                               
      568 +    let m = first.match(/^\*\*(.+?)\*\*/);          // **Bold 
          +headline**                                                    
      569 +    if (m) t = m[1];                                          
      570 +    if (!t) { m = first.match(/^#{1,4}\s+(.+)/); if (m) t = m[
          +1]; } // ### Heading

● Both issues resolved.

  "Untitled Article" — fixed (and prevented)

  Cause: generation resumed on 2026‑06‑25 (the neuron quota reset), and 27 new 
  articles came through titled "Untitled Article" — the model had stopped
  emitting the TITLE: label (it put the headline as the first bold line or a ###
  heading instead), so the parser fell back to the literal default.

  Fixed:
  - Backfilled all 27 from their own content/summary (e.g. "A New Era for
  Humanity: Pope Leo XIV's Call to Action", "The Sound of Bullets") → 0 untitled
  now.
  - Hardened the generator so it can't recur: parseArticleResponse now derives a
  real title from the content (first bold line → heading → first sentence)
  whenever TITLE: is missing, instead of writing "Untitled Article". Deployed.

  This also confirms good news from the other thread: the pipeline is generating
  again now that neurons are available — so the recovery/ordering work is
  paying off.

  The article-presentation audit (the task you set)

  I mapped every surface that renders articles and found one real defect plus
  confirmed the rest:

  - 🔴 Fixed — broken article links: the shared ArticleCard (used by the Saved 
  Library) and CountryHeroArticle linked to /articles/:slug, which has no route
  — clicks silently redirected to the homepage. Repointed both to the real
  /posts/:slug. (Deployed.)
  - Confirmed OK: Stories, Landing, Country hub, Feed, Search, Continental
  Overview, Narrative Toolkit all link correctly to /posts/ and render
  title/sector/country/reading‑time/hero consistently; the catalog data is clean
  (titles de‑asterisked, summaries present), and hero images fall back
  gracefully (editorial placeholders / SafeImage branded card / onError hide).
  - The remaining /articles/ references are API calls (correct backend path),
  not router links.

  Net: clicking any article from the Library now works, all eight sectors browse
  correctly, and no article will publish as "Untitled" again.
